\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
We study a class of subgraph and edge-based statistics in the random connection model generated by a homogeneous Poisson point process. These statistics are defined in terms of edge marks on pairs of Poisson points and are not themselves Poisson $U$-statistics. Our results extend existing limit theorems for Poisson $U$-statistics to geometric functionals defined on such edge-marked point processes. We show that these functionals can be approximated by Poisson $U$-statistics on suitable marked configuration spaces, which allows us to transfer limit theorems from the general theory of Poisson $U$-statistics to the random connection model and to extend them to certain non-Poisson $U$-statistics. In particular, we establish central limit theorems for subgraph counts, Poisson and stable limit theorems for edge-length functionals, as well as moderate deviation principles for the latter. Our results complement existing asymptotic results for the random connection model.


\end{abstract}
\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} $U$-statistics; point processes; random connection model; subgraph counts; edge-length functionals; central limit theorems; Poisson approximation; stable limit theorems.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{MSC 2020:} 05C80; 60D05; 60E07; 60F05; 60F17; 60G55. 

\section{Introduction}

Random graphs have been considered extensively for many years. For such graphs, whose vertices or edges are random, many models were introduced and a rich variety of properties and questions can be investigated, making random graphs an important topic of probability theory. Apart from their mathematical beauty, a further motivation to study random graphs is that they can be suitable models for complex networks. The latter are large graphs with a highly non-trivial structure as they arise in many applications ranging from social networks to biology and the engineering of telecommunication networks. For a detailed introduction to random graph and complex networks we refer, for example, to \cite{van_der_Hofstad_book}.

Many random graph models such as Erd\H{o}s-R\'enyi graphs, rank-one inhomogeneous random graphs, the configuration model or preferential attachment models are purely combinatorial in the sense that the set of vertices are usually the natural numbers $1,\hdots,n$. In contrast, the vertices of spatial random graphs are points in Euclidean space and the edges depend on the geometry of the points. For many complex networks such an assumption is reasonable since the position of a vertex in space could provide its features and, thus, be relevant for the existence of edges. An important model for spatial random graphs are random geometric graphs, which are also known as Gilbert graphs. Their vertices are given by the points of a Poisson process $\eta$ on some space $\mathbb{X}$ and two vertices $x,y\in\eta$ are connected by an edge whenever their distance does not exceed a given threshold $r$, i.e., $\|x-y\|\leq r$. Random geometric graphs are a much studied model; see, for example, the monograph \cite{Penrose_book}. For given vertices the edges of a random geometric graph are completely deterministic. This is generalised by the so-called random connection model where the edges between given vertices become random and the edge probability depends on the positions of the potential endpoints. The vertices of the random connection model are still the points of the Poisson process $\eta$ on a space $\mathbb{X}$ and moreover a symmetric function $\varphi:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,1]$, the so-called connection function, is chosen. For each pair of vertices $x,y\in\eta$ it is decided independently if they are connected by an edge and the probability of an edge is given by $\varphi(x,y)$.

The random connection model was first introduced in \cite{Penrose1}. Roughly speaking the mechanism for drawing edges in the random connection model can be seen as a mixture between the Erd\H{o}s-R\'enyi graph, where each vertex is present with the same probability, and the random geometric graph, where the edges are completely determined by the locations of the vertices.

An important class of random connection models are so-called weighted random connection models, where the vertices are equipped with i.i.d.\ weights that are also taken into account by the connection function. Such a class of random connection models was first studied in \cite{deprez2019scale}, who considered the continuous counterpart to a long-range percolation model on the lattice from \cite{deijfen2013scale}. \textcolor{blue}{\em Add more references on random connection model
}
Counting statistics on RCM gains vastly attention in recent years, including isolated vertices counts \cite{Penrose2}, component counts \cite{Last_Nestmann_Schulte} and subgraph counts in \cite{can2022, LiuPrivault,LiuPrivault25b},
with various approaches being proposed to analyze the structure and asymptotic properties of these random graphs.

For an at most countable collection of random elements $(X_i)_{i\in I}$ taking values in a space $\mathbb{Y}$ and a measurable function $f:\mathbb{Y}^k\to\mathbb{R}$ with $k\in\mathbb{N}$, the random variable
\[
\sum_{(i_1,\hdots,i_k)\in I^k_{\neq}} f(X_{i_1},\hdots,X_{i_k}),
\]
where $I^k_{\neq}$ denotes the set of all $k$-tuples of distinct elements of $I$, is called a $U$-statistic. In the classical setting one considers $n$ i.i.d.\ random elements $(X_i)_{i\in\{1,\hdots,n\}}$, a framework that has played a central role in the study of symmetric kernels and their limit theorems. However, this classical formulation restricts the kernel to depend solely on vertex attributes. As a consequence, it is often insufficient for capturing the structural dependencies inherent in random graphs and point processes.

To overcome this limitation, \cite{Janson91} introduced the notion of generalized $U$-statistics, which extend the classical definition by allowing the kernel to depend not only on vertex attributes but also on the edges between them. In their framework one considers sums of functions of the form
$$
f(X_{i_1},\hdots,X_{i_k},(Y_{i_j,i_\ell})_{j,\ell\in\{1,\dots,k\},\, j<\ell}),
$$
where $(X_i)_{i\in I}$ and $(Y_{i,j})_{i,j\in I,\,i<j}$ are two independent collections of i.i.d.\ random elements. This extension proved crucial for studying the asymptotic behaviour of subgraph counts and other graph-theoretic functionals, as it provides a unified framework for handling the dependencies induced by the underlying network structure. Further details can be found in Chapter~11 of the monograph \cite{janson}; see also the recent developments in \cite{zhangzs,bhattacharya23}.

In the context of the random connection model, it is therefore natural to adopt this generalized perspective. Rather than considering $k$-tuples of distinct points from a point process $\eta$ in isolation, one should also take into account the stochastic connectivity between them. Denote by $\eta^k_{\neq}$ the set of all $k$-tuples of distinct points of $\eta$, and write $\mathbf{1}\{x\leftrightarrow y\}$ for the indicator of the event that two points $x,y\in\eta$ are connected by an edge. For a measurable function $f:\mathbb{X}^k\times \{0,1\}^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}\to\mathbb{R}$,
we define the random variable
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:Ustatistic_RCM}
\sum_{(x_1,\hdots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\neq}}
f\big(x_1,\hdots,x_k,(\mathbf{1}\{x_i\leftrightarrow x_j\})_{1\le i<j\le k}\big)
\end{equation}
and call it a $U$-statistic of the random connection model. This formulation embeds the concept of generalized $U$-statistics into the continuum setting of the random connection model and provides a natural framework for analysing geometric and combinatorial characteristics of the resulting random graph.

Several prominent functionals of the random connection model can be framed as $U$-statistics. For example, consider the number of non-induced copies of a fixed connected graph $G$ with $k$ vertices, labelled $1,\hdots,k$ and edge set $E_G$. When restricted to an observation window $\widetilde{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathbb{X}$, this subgraph count is given by the $U$-statistic:
$$
\sum_{(x_1,\hdots,x_k)\in\eta^{k}_{\neq}\cap \widetilde{\Gamma}^k} \prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_G} \mathbf{1}\{x_i\leftrightarrow x_j\},
$$
The asymptotic behavior of such subgraph counts has been a central theme in random graph theory. In the contect of the random connection model, a central limit theorem was first established in \cite{can2022} under certain weak stabilization condition. The normal approximation for subgraph counts was derived in \cite{LiuPrivault} using the method of cumulant, and further refined in \cite{LiuPrivault25b}. Most recently, these limit theorems have been extended to the more general setting of  weighted random connection models in \cite{Heerten_Hirsch_Otto}.

Another class of $U$-statistics are the edge-length functionals
$$
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(x_1,x_2)\in\eta^2_{\neq}\cap \widetilde{\Gamma}^2} \mathbf{1}\{x_1\leftrightarrow x_2\} \|x_1-x_2\|^\tau.
$$
\textcolor{blue}{\em Add reference on edge-length functionals}


For a Poisson process $\tilde{\eta}$ in a space $\mathbb{Y}$ and a measurable function $f:\mathbb{Y}^k\to\mathbb{R}$, the $U$-statistic
$$
\sum_{(x_1,\hdots,x_k)\in\tilde{\eta}^k_{\neq}} f(x_1,\hdots,x_k)
$$
is called a Poisson $U$-statistic. Such random variables arise in many problems from stochastic geometry and have been studied intensively in recent years. For results on the normal approximation of Poisson $U$-statistics we refer to \cite{reitznerschulte,eichelsbacher,schulte}, while Poisson approximation and Poisson process approximation for related point processes was studied in \cite{dst, pianoforte}. Moderate deviation principles and concentration inequalities for Poisson $U$-statistics were considered in \cite{Bonnet_Gusakova,schulte2}.

Since the random connection model involves a Poisson process, it is a natural question whether results for Poisson $U$-statistics can be applied to its $U$-statistics. As introduced above the $U$-statistics of the random connection model are not of the desired form since apart from the underlying Poisson process $\eta$ for the vertices one has also the random decisions, which edges are present. This additional randomness can be modelled by marking each pair of vertices $\{x,y\}$ independently by a random variable $U_{\{x,y\}}$ that is uniformly distributed on $[0,1]$ and drawing an edge between $x$ and $y$ if and only if $U_{\{x,y\}}\leq\varphi(x,y)$. This way the random connection is completely determined by $\eta$ and $(U_{\{x,y\}})_{(x,y)\in\eta^2_{\neq}}$. Now the latter can be constructed from a marked Poisson process $\widehat{\eta}$, where the point process without the marks have the same distribution as $\eta$ and the marks are sequences of independent random variables uniformly distributed on $[0,1]$. The idea is to take the points of $\widehat{\eta}$ without marks as $\eta$ and to derive $(U_{\{x,y\}})_{(x,y)\in\eta^2_{\neq}}$ from the marks. To this end, one can equip $\mathbb{X}$ with an order $\prec$ and a distance ${\rm d}$. If $x\prec y$ and $y$ is the $j$-th closest point of $\eta$ to $x$, we let $U_{\{x,y\}}$ be the $j$-th element from the mark sequence of $x$. This means that the random connection model can be seen as a function of the Poisson process $\widehat{\eta}$. Nevertheless, a $U$-statistic of the random connection model does not become a Poisson $U$-statistic with respect to $\widehat{\eta}$. This is caused by the fact that the way the marks are assigned to the the pairs of vertices depends on all points of $\eta$ and thus destroys the structure of $U$-statistics.   

In this paper, we address the problem that $U$-statistics of the random connection model are not Poisson $U$-statistics. Our strategy is to approximate $U$-statistics of the random connection model by Poisson $U$-statistics with respect to Poisson processes on larger spaces. This way we can systematically transfer results for Poisson $U$-statistics to $U$-statistics of the random connection model. The key approximation argument in our proofs is the same that is employed in \cite{Last_Nestmann_Schulte} the derive second-order Poincar\'e inequalities for functionals of edge-marked Poisson processes.

\textcolor{blue}{Refer to erroneous applications in \cite{Heerten_Hirsch_Otto} and \cite{Otto25}}

We formulate our abstract results for the slightly more general framework of edge marked Poisson processes, where each pair of points of a Poisson process $\eta$ in $\mathbb{X}$ is equipped with an i.i.d.\ random mark in a space $\mathbb{M}$, i.e., one has a collection of so-called egde-marks $(\mathcal{M}_{x,y})_{x,y\in\eta}$. As described above the random connection model can be obtained from an edge-marked Poisson process with marks uniformly distributed on $[0,1]$. The definition of a $U$-statistic of an edge-marked Poisson process is the same as that of a $U$-statistic of a random connection model in \eqref{eqn:Ustatistic_RCM} with the only difference that the indicators of edges are replaced by the pairwise marks, i.e., for a measurable function $f:\mathbb{X}^k\times\mathbb{M}^{k(k-1)}$ we are interested in random variables of the form
$$
\sum_{(x_1,\hdots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\neq}} f(x_1,\hdots,x_k, (\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j\in\{1,\hdots,k\},i\neq j}).
$$ 
Under suitable assumption we derive for such random variables
\begin{itemize}
\item formulas for expectation and variance as well as for higher-order moments and cumulants
\item quantitative bounds for the normal approximation with respect to the Kolmogorov and the Wasserstein distance
\item a quantitative bound for Poisson approximation with respect to the Wasserstein distance
\item a quantitative bound for compound Poisson approximation with respect to the total variation distance
\item moderate deviation principles.
\end{itemize}
Apart from U-statistics we also consider some associated point processes. For a measurable function $\tilde{f}:\mathbb{X}^k\to\mathbb{Y}$ with $k\in\mathbb{N}$ and some space $\mathbb{Y}$, we can define a point process
$$
\tilde{\xi}:=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{(x_1,\hdots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\neq}} \delta_{\tilde{f}(x_1,\hdots,x_k)},
$$ 
where $\delta_y$ denotes the Dirac measure concentrated at a point $y\in\mathbb{Y}$. We also derive a quantitative bound for the Kantorovich-Rubinstein distance between $\tilde{\xi}$ and a Poisson process on $\mathbb{Y}$ under suitable assumptions. This result is employed to derive the Poisson and compound Poisson approximation bounds for $U$-statistics of the random connection model.

\textcolor{blue}{\em Comment on our applications, stationary Poisson process on Euclidean space}

\begin{equation} \label{g1}
 0< \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \varphi(x) \mathrm{d}x < \infty.
\end{equation}

Any two distinct points $x,y\in\eta$ are connected by an edge with probability $\varphi(x-y)$, independently of all other edges. \section{Results for $U$-statistics of random connection models}

In this section, we explore several applications on the RCM based on a stationary Poisson process on $\mathbb{R}^d$. Our analysis covers a variety of functionals, including subgraph counts, edge lengths and hub-to-hub edges. 

Let $\eta$ be a stationary Poisson process on $\mathbb{R}^d$ with intensity measure $\gamma\cdot \lambda_d$, where $\gamma>0$ is fixed and $\lambda_d$ denotes the Lebesgue measure on $\mathbb{R}^d$. 
Let $\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{R}^d$ be a compact convex set with $\lambda_d(\Gamma)=1$, and define $\Gamma_s:=s^{1/d}\Gamma$, for $s\ge1$. 

For any finite collection of points $\{x_1,\dots,x_r\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^d$, we denote the RCM formed on these points with connection function $\varphi$ as $\mathbb{G}_{\varphi}(\{x_1,\dots,x_r\})$. In this unweighted setting, any two distinct points $x,y\in\eta$ are connected be an edge independently with probability 
$$
\mathbb{P}(x\leftrightarrow y)=\varphi(x-y).
$$
This framework naturally extends to the weighted random connection model (WRCM), denoted $\mathbb{G}_{\varphi}(\{x_1,\dots,x_r\},W)$, by equipping each vertex with an i.i.d. weight drawn from a distribution $W$. In particular, we denote the Poisson RCM and its weighted counterpart base on the underlying Possion process $\eta$ as $\mathbb{G}_{\varphi}(\eta)$ and $\mathbb{G}_{\varphi}(\eta,W)$, respectively. 
\textcolor{cyan}{
\footnote{It should be moved to next section.}
Therefore, taking $\mathbb{M}=[0,1]$, and $\mathbb{Q}$ to be the uniform distribution on $\mathbb{M}$, we have
\begin{equation*}
	\mathbf{1}(x\leftrightarrow y)\overset{d}{=}\mathbf{1}(\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j}\le\varphi(x_i-x_j)).
\end{equation*}
}
To establish our limit theorems, we require precise measures of the distance between random elements. 
We investigate the closeness of both real-valued random variables and point processes using several probability metrics.

%In this paper, we investigate the closeness between two real-valued random variables, as well as between two point processes. To this end, we employ several probability distances, including the Kolmogorov distance, the Wasserstein distance, and the Kantorovich--Rubinstein distance.
For real-valued random variables $X$ and $Y$, we employ the Kolmogorov distance,
\begin{equation*}
	\mathrm{d}_{\rm K}(X,Y):=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathbb{P}(X\le z)-\mathbb{P}(Y\le z)|,
\end{equation*}
and the Wasserstein distance,
\begin{equation*}
	\mathrm{d}_{\rm Wass}(X,Y):=\sup_{h\in\mathscr{F}_W}|\mathbb{E} h(X)-\mathbb{E} h(Y)|,
\end{equation*}
where $\mathscr{F}_W:=\{h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}:~|h(x)-h(y)|\le |x-y|\}$.

When comparing the global structure of random graphs, it is often convenient to treat the graph (or its functionals) as point processes. 
Let $\mathbb{Y}$ be a locally compact second countable Hausdorff space with Borel $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{Y}$.
Let $N_{\mathbb{Y}}$ denote the space of $\sigma$-finite counting measures on $\mathbb{Y}$. Let $\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{Y}}$ be the $\sigma$-algebra on $N_{\mathbb{Y}}$ generated by evalution mappings $\omega\mapsto\omega(A)$, $A\in\mathcal{Y}$.
A point process $\xi$ is then a random element in $N_{\mathbb{Y}}$.

To compare two point processes $\xi$ and $\zeta$ with distribution $P$ and $Q$, we utilize the Kantorovich-Rubinstein distance:
\begin{equation*}
	\mathrm{d}_{\rm KR}(\xi,\zeta):= \inf_{C\in\Sigma(P,Q)}\int_{N_{\mathbb{Y}}\times N_{\mathbb{Y}}}\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\omega_1,\omega_2) C(\mathrm{d}(\omega_1,\omega_2)),
\end{equation*}
where $\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\nu_1,\nu_2):=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{Y}}|\nu_1(A)-\nu_2(A)|$ is the total variation distance between finite measures, and 
$\Sigma(P,Q)$ is the set of probability measures on $N_{\mathbb{Y}}\times N_{\mathbb{Y}}$ with first marginal $P$ and second marginal $Q$.

%\end{equation*}

\subsection{Subgraph counts}
Let $G=(V_G,E_G)$ be a fixed connected graph of order $k$, $k\ge2$, with vertex set $V_G$ and edge set $E_G$. 
%For any two graphs $G_1$ and $G_2$, we denote $G_1\simeq G_2$ when $G_1$ is isomorphic to $G_2$. 
Denote $|\mathrm{Aut}(G)|$ as the number of automorphism of $G$. 
Define $v(G):=|V_G|$ as the number of vertices and $e(G):=|E_G|$ as the number of edges of the graph $G$. 
For any two graphs $G_1,G_2$, we write $G_1\simeq G_2$ when $G_1$ is isomorphic to $G_2$. 
\begin{defi}{\cite[pp.~64-65]{JLR}}
 % \label{balgra}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1)]
A graph $G$ is balanced if 
\begin{equation}
  \frac{e(H)}{v(H)}\leq \frac{e(G)}{v(G)},
  \qquad \quad \text{for all }H\subseteq G,   \label{balance1} 
    \end{equation}
and strictly balanced if \eqref{balance1} 
holds as a strict inequality for all $H\subsetneq G$. 
\item[2)] A graph $G$ is $K_1$-balanced, also called strongly balanced, if 
\begin{equation}
	\frac{e(H)}{v(H)-1}\le\frac{e(G)}{v(G)-1}, \qquad \quad \text{for all }H\subseteq G \quad \text{with} \quad v(H)>1,
	\label{balance2}
\end{equation}
with the convention $0/0=0$.
\end{itemize}
  \end{defi}

We consider $\ell$ copies of $G$ with the vertices, denoted by $x_i^{(r)}$ for $i \in [k]$ and $r \in [1, \dots, \ell]$. For a given partition $\pi \in \widetilde{\Pi}^\ell(k)$, the {\it contracted graph} $(G^{\otimes \ell})_{\pi}$ is constructed as follows:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Vertices:} The vertex set consists of the blocks of the partition $\pi$. Formally, two vertices $x_{i_1}^{(r_1)}$ and $x_{i_2}^{(r_2)}$ are identified as a single vertex in $(G^{\otimes \ell})_{\pi}$ if and only if the indices $(r_1, i_1)$ and $(r_2, i_2)$ belong to the same block of $\pi$. The resulting graph has exactly $|\pi|$ vertices.
    
    \item \textbf{Edges:} The edge set is inherited from the $\ell$ copies of $G$. For every original edge $\{i, j\} \in E_G$, we include the edges $\{x_i^{(r)}, x_j^{(r)}\}$ for all $r \in [\ell]$, subject to the vertex identifications defined by $\pi$.
\end{itemize}
Note that Because $G$ is connected and the identification process (contraction) does not break existing paths, the resulting graph $(G^{\otimes \ell})_{\pi}$ remains connected.
\subsubsection{Subgraph counts with integrable connection function}
Suppose the connection function $\varphi$ satisfies 
$$
\int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\varphi(x)\mathrm{d}x<\infty.
$$
To ensure normal approximation, we need the following minimal condition for the connection function $\varphi$.
\begin{ass}\label{graph-ass1}
	Let $G$ be a connected graph with $k\ge2$ vertices.
\begin{enumerate}
	\item[i)] 
\begin{equation}
	\mathbb{P}(\exists\ H\subseteq \mathbb{G}_{\varphi}(\eta):\ H\simeq G)>0.
\end{equation}
\item[ii)]
\begin{equation}
	\mathbb{P}\left(\exists\ \{x_1,\dots,x_k\}\subseteq\eta:\ \mathbb{G}_{\varphi}(\{x_1,\dots,x_k\})\simeq G\right)>0.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{ass}
\begin{theorem}\label{propgraph}
Let $G$ be a connected graph with $k\ge2$ vertices.
Denote by $N_G^{\subset}$ and $N_G^{=}$ the non-induced subgraph count and induced subgraph counts, respectively, of copies of $G$ in 
$\mathbb{G}_{\varphi}(\eta\cap\Gamma_s)$, defined by
\begin{align}
	N_G^{\subset}&:=\frac1{|\mathrm{Aut}(G)|}\sum_{(x_1,\dots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\ne}\cap\Gamma_s^k}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_G}\mathbf{1}\{x_i\leftrightarrow x_j\},\label{def:subgraph-count1}\\
	N_G^{=}&:=\frac1{|\mathrm{Aut}(G)|}\sum_{(x_1,\dots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\ne}\cap\Gamma_s^k}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_G}
    \mathbf{1}\{x_i\leftrightarrow x_j\}\prod_{\{\imath,\jmath\}\notin E_G}\mathbf{1}\{x_{\imath}\nleftrightarrow x_{\jmath}\}.\label{def:subgraph-count2}
\end{align}
If Assumption~\ref{graph-ass1}-i) holds, then there exist constants $C,s_0>0$, depending only on $\varphi$, $k$ and $G$ such that when $s\ge s_0$,
\begin{equation}
	\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{Wass}}\left(\frac{N_G^{\subset}-\mathbb{E} N_G^{\subset}}{\sqrt{\Var N_G^{\subset}}},N\right)\le \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{K}}\left(\frac{N_G^{\subset}-\mathbb{E} N_G^{\subset}}{\sqrt{\Var N_G^{\subset}}},N\right)\le\frac{C}{\sqrt{s}}.
\end{equation}
Moreover, if Assumption~\ref{graph-ass1}-ii) holds, the same bound applies to the induced subgraph count $N_G^{=}$.
\end{theorem}
It can be shown that, 
as a direct application of Proposition~\ref{prop:moments}, the growth of cumulants of subgraph counts is well controlled, which is essential for normal approximation in Theorem~\ref{propgraph}.
\begin{prop}\label{subgraph-cumu-1}
For any $\ell\in\mathbb{N}$ and $i\in\{=,\subset\}$, we have, for each $\pi\in\widetilde{\Pi}^\ell(k)$
\begin{equation}\label{intgrand-0}
\int_{\Gamma_s^{|\pi|}}\int_{[0,1]^{|\pi|\times|\pi|}_{\ne}}((f^{(i)}_G)^{\otimes\ell})_{\pi}(\bm{x},\bm{u})\mathrm{d}\bm{u}\lambda_d^{|\pi|}(\mathrm{d}\bm{x})\le 
\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(G)|^\ell}\lambda_d(\Gamma_s)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\varphi(z)\mathrm{d}z\right)^{|\pi|-1}\gamma^{|\pi|}.
\end{equation}
Moreover, we have 
\begin{equation}
	\cum_\ell(N_G^{(i)})\le \frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(G)|^\ell}\left[\left(\gamma \int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\varphi(z)\mathrm{d}z\right)\vee1\right]^{\ell(k-1)+1}k^{k\ell}(\ell!)^k s.
\end{equation}
 \end{prop}

As a directly consequence of cumulants estimates established in Proposition~\ref{subgraph-cumu-1} and the proof of Theorem~\ref{propgraph}, we obtain moderate deviation principle, concentration inequality and normal approximation with Cram\'er correction for the normalized subgraph counts via the method of cumulants. 

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:subgraphMDP}
	Let $G$ be a connected graph with $k\ge2$ vertices, and $\{s_n\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ be a positive real-valued sequence with $s_n\to\infty$. 
Denote by $N_n^{\subset}$ and $N_n^{=}$ the non-induced subgraph count and induced subgraph counts, respectively, of copies of $G$ in 
$\mathbb{G}_{\varphi}(\eta\cap\Gamma_{s_n})$, defined by
\begin{align}
	N_n^{\subset}&:=\frac1{|\mathrm{Aut}(G)|}\sum_{(x_1,\dots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\ne}\cap\Gamma_{s_n}^k}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_G}\mathbf{1}\{x_i\leftrightarrow x_j\},\\
	N_n^{=}&:=\frac1{|\mathrm{Aut}(G)|}\sum_{(x_1,\dots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\ne}\cap\Gamma_{s_n}^k}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_G}
    \mathbf{1}\{x_i\leftrightarrow x_j\}\prod_{\{\imath,\jmath\}\notin E_G}\mathbf{1}\{x_{\imath}\nleftrightarrow x_{\jmath}\}.
\end{align}
If Assumption~\ref{graph-ass1}-i) holds, then there exists constant $C$ (see \eqref{mdp-const1}) such that  
$\{N_n^{\subset}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ satisfies 
\textbf{$\mathrm{MDP}$}$(k-1,\{C\sqrt{s_n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}})$, \textbf{$\mathrm{CI}$}$(k-1, \{C\sqrt{s_n}\}_{n \in \mathbb{N}})$, and \textbf{$\mathrm{NACC}$}$(k-1,\{C\sqrt{s_n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}})$. 
Moreover, if Assumption~\ref{graph-ass1}-ii) holds, then 
$\{N_n^{=}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ satisfies 
\textbf{$\mathrm{MDP}$}$(k-1,\{C\sqrt{s_n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}})$, \textbf{$\mathrm{CI}$}$(k-1, \{C\sqrt{s_n}\}_{n \in \mathbb{N}})$, and \textbf{$\mathrm{NACC}$}$(k-1,\{C\sqrt{s_n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}})$. 
\end{theorem}
\subsubsection{Subgraph counts with non-integrable connection function}

\begin{ass}\label{bound-ass-1}
The connection function $\varphi$ is radial with radial profile $\bar{\varphi}$. The function $\bar{\varphi}$ is non-increasing and there exist constants $l_{\bar{\varphi}}\in(0,\infty)$ and $\alpha\in(0,d)$ such that
\begin{equation}
\lim_{t\to\infty} t^{\alpha} \bar{\varphi}(t)=l_{\bar{\varphi}}.
\end{equation}
\end{ass}
Before presenting the main results of this section, we first illustrate the key ideas with a toy example.
\begin{example}
	When $G=K_3$ is a triangle, according to Proposition~\ref{prop:expectation_variance}, we have
\begin{align*}
\Var(N_{K_3}^{\subset})=9I_1(s)+18I_2(s)+3I_3(s),
\end{align*}
where
\begin{align*}
	I_1(s)&:=\gamma^5\int_{\Gamma_s^5}\varphi(x_1-x_2)\varphi(x_2-x_3)\varphi(x_3-x_1)\varphi(x_1-x_4)\varphi(x_1-x_5)\varphi(x_4-x_5)\mathrm{d}(x_1,\dots,x_5),\\
	I_2(s)&:=\gamma^4\int_{\Gamma_s^4}\varphi(x_1-x_2)\varphi(x_2-x_3)\varphi(x_3-x_1)\varphi(x_1-x_4)\varphi(x_4-x_2)\mathrm{d}(x_1,\dots,x_4),\\
	I_3(s)&:=\gamma^3\int_{\Gamma_s^3}\varphi(x_1-x_2)\varphi(x_2-x_3)\varphi(x_3-x_1)\mathrm{d}(x_1,x_2,x_3).
\end{align*}

Moreover, by letting $v_i:=s^{-1/d}x_i$, we can write 
\begin{align*}
	I_1(s)&=\gamma^5\int_{\Gamma^5}\varphi(s^{1/d}(v_1-v_2))\varphi(s^{1/d}(v_2-v_3))\varphi(s^{1/d}(v_3-v_1))\\
	      &\ \ \ \times\varphi(s^{1/d}(v_1-v_4))\varphi(s^{1/d}(v_1-v_5))\varphi(s^{1/d}(v_4-v_5))s^{5}\mathrm{d}(v_1,\dots,v_5),\\
	      &\asymp s^5 s^{-6\frac{\alpha}d}\int_{\Gamma^5}\|v_1-v_2\|^{-\alpha}\|v_2-v_3\|^{-\alpha}\|v_3-v_1\|^{-\alpha}\\
	      &\ \ \ \times\|v_1-v_4\|^{-\alpha}\|v_1-v_5\|^{-\alpha}\|v_4-v_5\|^{-\alpha}\mathrm{d}(v_1,\dots,v_5).
\end{align*}
Because $\varphi$ is bounded by $1$, integrations over $\Gamma$ is finite. Hence, we have 
\begin{equation}
	I_1(s)\asymp s^{5-6\alpha/d},~~I_2(s)\asymp s^{4-5\alpha/d},~~I_3(s)\asymp s^{3-3\alpha/d},
\end{equation}
and 
\begin{equation}
	\Var(N_{K_3}^{\subset})\asymp
\begin{cases}
	s^{5-6\alpha/d},\ \ \ &\mbox{when}~\alpha\in(0,\frac23d],\\
	s^{3-3\alpha/d},\ \ \ &\mbox{when}~\alpha\in(\frac23d,d).
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
\end{example}

\begin{lemma}\label{noninteg-upper-1}
If Assumption~\ref{bound-ass-1} holds, we have, for any $\ell\in\mathbb{N}$ and  $\pi\in\widetilde{\Pi}^\ell(k)$
\begin{equation}\label{noninteg-upper-2}
\int_{\Gamma_s^{|\pi|}}\int_{[0,1]^{|\pi|\times|\pi|}_{\ne}}((f^{\subset}_G)^{\otimes\ell})_{\pi}(\bm{x},\bm{u})\mathrm{d}\bm{u}\lambda_d^{|\pi|}(\mathrm{d}\bm{x})\asymp 
s^{|\pi|-e((G^{\otimes\ell})_\pi)\alpha/d}.
\end{equation}
Moreover, suppose that $G$ is $K_1$-balanced, we have 
\begin{align}
	\label{cumasym1}
    \cum_\ell(N_G^{\subset})\asymp
\begin{cases}
	s^{1+(k-1)\ell}\cdot s^{-\ell e(G)\alpha/d}, \ \ \ &\mbox{when}~\alpha\in(0,\frac{k-1}{e(G)}d],\\
	s^{k-e(G)\alpha/d}, \ \ \ &\mbox{when}~\alpha\in(\frac{k-1}{e(G)}d,d).
\end{cases}
\end{align}
 \end{lemma}
%}
\begin{remark}
	All trees, cycles, and complete graphs are $K_1$-balanced, see \cite{RucinskiVince86}. 
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
The assumption that $G$ is $K_1$-balanced is necessary to guarantee the dichotomy in \eqref{cumasym1}; see \cite[Proposition~6.1]{LiuPrivault25b} for a precise statement and proof.
\end{remark}
\iffalse
\begin{lemma}\label{noninte-lower-1}
If Assumption~\ref{bound-ass-1} holds, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{lowerbound-2}
	\Var(N_G^{\subset})\gtrsim s^{2k-1-2|E_G|\alpha/d}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For the convex set $\Gamma_s$, we denote the inradius of $\Gamma_s$ as $r(\Gamma_s)$. Given that the volume of $\Gamma_s$ is $s$, there exists a constant $c_1>0$ such that  $r(\Gamma_s)\le c_1 s^{1/d}$. Since $\bar{\varphi}(r)\asymp r^{-\alpha}$ is non-increasing, there exists a constant $c_2>0$ such that for all sufficiently large $s$  
\begin{equation}
	\inf_{x,y\in\Gamma_s}\varphi(x-y)=\inf_{0\le t\le 2r(\Gamma_s)}\bar{\varphi}(t)\ge c_2 r(\Gamma_s)^{-\alpha}\asymp s^{-\alpha/d}.
\end{equation}
The proof follows from that there exists a spanning tree in each connected graph, as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{subgraph-cumu-1}.
Take $\rho \in \widetilde{\Pi}^2(k)$ to be the set partition such that 
$\{(1,1),(2,1)\} \in \rho$ and every other block of $\rho$ contains exactly one element, 
so that $|\rho| = 2k - 1$. 
Consequently, there exists a spanning tree $I_{\rho} \in \mathcal{I}_{2k-1}$ of 
$(G^{\otimes 2})_{\rho}$.
Hence, we have 
\begin{align*}
&\Var(N_G^{\subset})\\
&=\sum_{\pi\in\widetilde{\Pi}^2(k)}\int_{\Gamma_s^{|\pi|}}\int_{[0,1]^{|\pi|(|\pi|-1)/2}}(f^{\subset}_G\otimes f^{\subset}_G)_{\pi}(\bm{x},\bm{u})\mathrm{d}\bm{u}\gamma^{|\pi|}\mathrm{d}\bm{x}\nonumber\\
&\ge\int_{(\mathbb{R}^d)^{2k-1}}\mathbf{1}(x_1,\dots,x_{2k-1}\in\Gamma_s)\left(\prod_{\{i,j\}\in E_{(G^{\otimes 2})_{\rho}}}\varphi(x_i-x_j)\right)\mathrm{d}(x_1,\dots,x_{2k-1})\\
&\ge\int_{(\mathbb{R}^d)^{2k-1}}\mathbf{1}(x_1,\dots,x_{2k-1}\in\Gamma_s)\left(\prod_{\{i,j\}\in I_{\rho}}\varphi(x_i-x_j)\right)\mathrm{d}(x_1,\dots,x_{2k-1})
\times\left[\inf_{x,y\in\Gamma_s}\varphi(x-y)\right]^{2|E_G|-(2k-2)}\\
&\gtrsim s \left(s^{1-\alpha/d}\right)^{2k-2}\left(s^{-\alpha/d}\right)^{2|E_G|-(2k-2)}=s^{2k-1-2|E_G|\alpha/d}.
\end{align*}
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
	Note the lower bound in \eqref{lowerbound-2} is sharp, meaning it coincides with the upper bound established in \eqref{upperbound-2},  when $|E_G|=k-1$, i.e., when $G$ is a tree.
\end{remark}
\fi

From Lemma~\ref{noninteg-upper-1} and Theorem~\ref{normalapp1}, the following result is straightforward. 
\begin{theorem}
Assuming that Assumption~\ref{bound-ass-1} holds and $G$ is $K_1$-balanced, we have
\begin{align}
	\label{norm-opt}
	\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{Wass}}\left(\frac{N_G^{\subset}-\mathbb{E} N_G^{\subset}}{\sqrt{\Var N_G^{\subset}}},N\right)\le \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{K}}\left(\frac{N_G^{\subset}-\mathbb{E} N_G^{\subset}}{\sqrt{\Var N_G^{\subset}}},N\right)\le\frac{C}{\Delta_s},
\end{align}
with 
\begin{align}\label{non-integrable-rate0}
\Delta_s:=
\begin{cases}
	\displaystyle
	\sqrt{s}, \ \ \ &\mbox{when}~\alpha\in(0,\frac{k-1}{e(G)}d],\\
	\displaystyle
	\sqrt{s^{k-e(G)\alpha/d}}, \ \ \ &\mbox{when}~\alpha\in\left(\frac{k-1}{e(G)}d,(\frac{k}{e(G)}\wedge 1)d\right).
\end{cases}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}
\begin{remark}
	The convergence rates established in \cite[Theorem~3.5]{LiuPrivault25b} are of order $O(\Delta_s^{-1/(2k-1)})$, where $\Delta_s$ is defined as in \eqref{non-integrable-rate0}. In contrast, our estimates in \eqref{norm-opt} provide what are conjected to be optimal rates of convergence. 
\end{remark}


%\subsection{edge-length functionals}
\subsection{Edge-lengths}

\begin{ass}\label{edgelength-ass1}
	The function $\varphi$ is only radially dependent. Its radial part is denoted by $\bar{\varphi}$, that has a polynomial decay with some exponent $\alpha \in (d, \infty)$, i.e. there exists a constant $l_{\bar{\varphi}}\in(0,\infty)$ such that  
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:assumption_phi}
\lim_{t\to\infty} t^{\alpha} \bar{\varphi}(t)=l_{\bar{\varphi}}.
\end{equation}
\end{ass}

Note that the condition $\alpha>d$ in Assumption \ref{edgelength-ass1} ensures that the (non-trivial) integrability property
$$ 0 < \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \varphi (x) \, \mathrm{d} x< \infty$$
is satisfied.
Together with the observation that $\bar{\varphi}$ as a probability is bounded by one, this yields the existence of a constant $C_{\bar{\varphi}}\in(0,\infty)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:Upper_bound_bar_varphi}
\bar{\varphi}(t) \leq \frac{C_{\bar{\varphi}}}{t^\alpha}
\end{equation}
for all $t\in(0,\infty)$. Moreover, we define
$$
c_{\bar{\varphi}} := \frac{\gamma^2 d \kappa_d l_{\bar{\varphi}}}{2(\alpha-d)}.
$$

In the following, we first consider the point processes
$$
\tilde{\xi}_s := \frac{1}{2} \sum_{(x_1,x_2)\in\eta_{\neq}^2} \mathbf{1}\bigg\{x_1\leftrightarrow x_2, \frac{x_1+x_2}{2}\in\Gamma_s \bigg\} \delta_{s^{-\frac{1}{\alpha-d}}\|x_1-x_2\|}
$$
for $s\geq 1$, and also the functional form statistics 
\begin{equation}\label{deflstau}
    L_s (\tau):=\frac{1}{2} \sum_{(z_1, z_2) \in \eta^2_{\neq} \cap \Gamma_s^2} \mathbf{1}\{ z_1 \leftrightarrow z_2 \} \| z_1- z_2 \|^\tau, 
\end{equation}
for $\tau \in \mathbb{R}$ and $s\ge1$.

Alternatively, one could also consider a modified version that eliminates boundary effects:
\[
\widetilde{L}_s(\tau)
:= \frac{1}{2} \sum_{(z_1, z_2) \in \eta^2_{\neq}}
   \mathbf{1}\!\left\{
      \tfrac{z_1 + z_2}{2} \in \Gamma_s,\;
      z_1 \leftrightarrow z_2
   \right\}
   \| z_1 - z_2 \|^{\tau}.
\]
Here, the summation runs over all pairs of points in $\eta$ whose {midpoint}
lies inside $\Gamma_s$, regardless of whether both endpoints belong to $\Gamma_s$.
%This statistic is the \emph{midpoint version} of the edge-length sum. 
Here we focus on $L_s(\tau)$. The presence of boundary effects leads to different scaling properties and limit theorems.

We first state the following result regarding the point process $\tilde{\xi}_s$.



\begin{theorem}\label{thm:edge-length_process}
Assume that \eqref{eqn:assumption_phi} is satisfied with $\alpha\in(d,\infty)$. Let $\zeta$ be a homogeneous Poisson process on $(0,\infty)$ with intensity $c_{\bar{\varphi}}$ and let
$$
\zeta^{(\alpha)}:= \sum_{t\in\zeta} \delta_{t^{-\frac{1}{\alpha-d}}}.
$$
\begin{itemize}
\item [a)] For any $a\in(0,\infty)$,
$$
\mathrm{d}_{\rm KR}(\tilde{\xi}_s|_{[a,\infty)},\zeta^{(\alpha)}|_{[a,\infty)}) \leq \frac{\gamma^2 d \kappa_d }{2}\int_a^\infty \big|s^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-d}}v^{\alpha} \bar{\varphi}(s^{\frac{1}{\alpha-d}}v) -l_{\bar{\varphi}}\big| \, v^{d-\alpha-1}\, \mathrm{d}v + \frac{4\gamma^3 (d\kappa_d C_{\bar{\varphi}})^2}{(\alpha-d)^2 a^{2(\alpha-d)}} \frac{1}{s}.
$$
Moreover, the right-hand side vanishes as $s\to\infty$.
\item [b)] One has
$$
\tilde{\xi}_s\overset{d}{\longrightarrow} \zeta^{(\alpha)}\quad \text{as} \quad s\to\infty.
$$
\item [c)] 
Let $Z$ be a random variable such that $\mathbb{P}(Z>a)=e^{-c_{\bar{\varphi}}a^{d-\alpha}}$ for $a\in(0,\infty)$. Then,
$$
s^{-\frac{1}{\alpha-d}}\max_{(x_1,x_2)\in\eta_{\neq}^2, x_1\leftrightarrow x_2, \frac{x_1+x_2}{2}\in\Gamma_s} \|x_1-x_2\| \overset{d}{\longrightarrow} Z \quad \text{as} \quad s\to\infty.
$$
\end{itemize}
\end{theorem}

The following is the main result for the behavior of power-weighted edge-lengths. In particular we get different limit theorems (central or stable) depending on the values of $\alpha$, $d$ and $\tau$, including a distinctive regime with $\alpha =2d$, where a different limit theorem holds.

\begin{theorem}\label{mainsumedges}
    Let $L_s (\tau)$ be defined as in \eqref{deflstau} with $\tau>-d/2$. Suppose that Assumption \ref{edgelength-ass1} is satisfied. Then, as $s\to\infty$, the following trichotomy holds.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=2.2em]
\item {Gaussian regime.}
If either
\[
\alpha \ge 2\tau+d
\qquad\text{or}\qquad
\alpha\in(d,2d),
\]
then
\[
\frac{L_s(\tau)-\E[L_s(\tau)]}{\sqrt{\Var(L_s(\tau))}}
\overset{d}{\longrightarrow} N.
\]

\item {Stable regime.}
If
\[
2d<\alpha<2\tau+d,
\]
then there exist centring constants $b_s\in\mathbb{R}$ and scaling constants $a_s>0$ such that
\[
\frac{L_s(\tau)-b_s}{a_s}
\overset{d}{\longrightarrow} S_{\kappa},
\qquad
\kappa:=\frac{\alpha-d}{\tau}\in(0,2),
\]
where $S_{\kappa}$ is a non-degenerate $\kappa$-stable law.

\item {Distinctive regime.}
If
\[
\alpha=2d
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\alpha<2\tau+d,
\]
then a distinctive, non-Gaussian and non-stable limit theorem holds, as specified in
Theorem~\ref{thm:stable}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

Figure \ref{fig:phase-diagram-tau-alpha} summarizes the findings of Theorem \ref{mainsumedges}.

\caption{Schematic phase diagram in the $(\tau,\alpha)$-plane.
The grey region indicates parameter regimes in which a central limit theorem holds for the sums of power-weighted edge lengths.
The green line $\alpha=2\tau+d$ marks a critical regime in which a central limit theorem still holds. The lighter grey region corresponds to regimes with stable (non-Gaussian) limit theorems.
Finally, the red line $\alpha=2d$ highlights a boundary regime with a distinct limiting behaviour.}
\label{fig:phase-diagram-tau-alpha}

\end{figure}


Section~\ref{regimeclt} contains the proofs of the central limit theorems, whereas Section~\ref{nongaussianregime} provides the proofs of the non-Gaussian limit theorems, covering both the stable regime and the critical (distinctive) case. Moreover, in partial regimes for the central limit theorem, we additionally provide upper bounds on the convergence rates.






\subsection{Hub-to-hub edges}

In the following we study the weighted random connection model such that
$$
\mathbb{P}(x\leftrightarrow y) = \varphi(\kappa(W_x,W_y)/\|x-y\|^d)
$$
for given $x,y\in\eta$, $x\neq y$, with weights $W_x$ and $W_y$. Here, $\kappa: [0,\infty)^2\to[0,\infty)$ is measurable and $\varphi:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)$ satisfies
$$
c_\varphi:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \varphi(1/\|v\|^d) \, \mathrm{d}v <\infty.
$$

In the following, we are interested in edges whose endpoints have large weights. We denote such edges as hub-to-hub edges. Let $(t_s)_{s\geq 1}$ be a family of non-negative real numbers. For $s\geq 1$ we study
$$
E_s:= \frac{1}{2} \sum_{(x_1,x_2)\in\eta^2_{\neq}} \mathbf{1}\{ x_1\leftrightarrow x_2 \} \mathbf{1}\bigg\{ \frac{x_1+x_2}{2}\in \Gamma_s, W_{x_1},W_{x_2} \geq t_s \bigg\},
$$
i.e., we count all edges with vertices having their midpoint in the observation window and weights exceeding $t_s$.

We have that
$$
\mathbb{E}[E_s] = \frac{\gamma^2}{2} \mathbb{E} \int_{(\mathbb{R}^d)^2} \mathbf{1}\bigg\{ \frac{x_1+x_2}{2}\in \Gamma_s \bigg\} \mathbf{1}\{W_1,W_2\geq t_s\}  \varphi(\kappa(W_1,W_2)/\|x_1-x_2\|^d) \, \mathrm{d}(x_1,x_2)
$$
with independent copies $W_1$ and $W_2$ of the typical weight. The substitutions $z=(x_1+x_2)/2$ and $u=x_1-x_2$ as well as $u'=u/\kappa(W_1,W_2)^{1/d}$ lead to
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{E}[E_s] & = \frac{\gamma^2}{2} \mathbb{E} \int_{(\mathbb{R}^d)^2} \mathbf{1}\{z\in \Gamma_s\} \mathbf{1}\{W_1,W_2\geq t_s\}  \varphi(\kappa(W_1,W_2)/\|u\|^d) \, \mathrm{d}(z,u) \\
& =  \frac{\gamma^2 \lambda_d(\Gamma_s)}{2} \mathbb{E}\bigg[ \mathbf{1}\{W_1,W_2\geq t_s\}  \int_{\mathbb{R}^d}  \varphi(\kappa(W_1,W_2)/\|u\|^d) \, \mathrm{d}u \bigg] \\
& =  \frac{\gamma^2 s}{2} \mathbb{E}\bigg[ \mathbf{1}\{W_1,W_2\geq t_s\}  \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \kappa(W_1,W_2) \varphi(1/\|u'\|^d) \, \mathrm{d}u' \bigg] \\
& = \frac{\gamma^2 s}{2} c_\varphi \mathbb{E}[\kappa(W_1,W_2) \mathbf{1}\{W_1,W_2\geq t_s\}].
\end{align*}

For $E_s$ we can derive the following Poisson and normal approximation results.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:hub-to-hub-edges}
Denote by $W_1,\hdots,W_5$ independent copies of $W$.
\begin{itemize}
\item [a)] Assume that $\mathbb{E}[\kappa(W_1,W_2)]<\infty$ and $\mathbb{E}[\kappa(W_1,W_2) \kappa(W_1,W_3)]<\infty$. For $s\geq 1$ and $Z_s\sim\operatorname{Poisson}(\gamma^2 c_\varphi s \mathbb{E}[\kappa(W_1,W_2) \mathbf{1}\{W_1,W_2\geq t_s\}]/2)$, one has
$$
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{Wass}}(E_s, Z_s) \leq 4 \gamma^3 c_\varphi^2 s \mathbb{E}[ \mathbf{1}\{ W_1,W_2,W_3\geq t_s \} \kappa(W_1,W_2) \kappa(W_1,W_3) ].
$$
\item [b)] Assume that
$$
\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=2}^i \kappa(W_1,W_j) \bigg]<\infty \quad \quad \text{for} \quad i\in\{4,5\}
$$
and
$$
\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^i \kappa(W_j,W_{j+1})\bigg]<\infty \quad \text{for} \quad i\in\{1,\hdots,4\}
$$
let $N$ be a standard normal random variable.
Then, there exist constants $C_{\mathrm{Wass}},C_{\mathrm{K}}\in(0,\infty)$ only depending on $\gamma$ and $\varphi$ such that, for $s\geq 1$,
$$
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{Wass}}\bigg( \frac{E_s-\mathbb{E}[E_s]}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}[E_s]}}, N \bigg) \leq \frac{C_{\mathrm{Wass}}  R_s}{\sqrt{s} \mathbb{E}[\kappa(W_1,W_2) \mathbf{1}\{W_1,W_2\geq t_s\}]}
$$
and
$$
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{K}}\bigg( \frac{E_s-\mathbb{E}[E_s]}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}[E_s]}}, N \bigg) \leq \frac{C_{\mathrm{K}}  R_s}{\sqrt{s} \mathbb{E}[\kappa(W_1,W_2) \mathbf{1}\{W_1,W_2\geq t_s\}]}
$$
with
\begin{align*}
R_s & := \sum_{i=4}^5 \sqrt{\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=2}^i \kappa(W_1,W_j) \mathbf{1}\{W_1,\hdots,W_{i+1}\geq t_s\}\bigg]} \\
& \quad +\sum_{i=1}^4 \sqrt{\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^i \kappa(W_j,W_{j+1})\mathbf{1}\{W_1,\hdots,W_{i+1}\geq t_s\}\bigg]}.
\end{align*}
\end{itemize}
\end{theorem}
For $s\to\infty$ one should use Poisson approximation for $E_s$ if $\mathbb{E}[E_s]$ converges to some constant and normal approximation if $\mathbb{E}[E_s]\to\infty$.

As an application of Theorem \ref{thm:hub-to-hub-edges} we consider the situation of the so-called product kernel $\kappa(w_1,w_2)=w_1w_2$, which is a very prominent choice for $\kappa$ and where our formulas simpliy.

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:hub-to-hub-edges1}
Suppose that $\kappa(w_1,w_2)=w_1w_2$.
\begin{itemize}
\item [a)] Assume that $\mathbb{E}[W^2]<\infty$. Let $(t_s)_{s\geq 1}$ be such that $\sqrt{s}\mathbb{E}[W\mathbf{1}\{W\geq t_s\}]=a$ for some $a\in(0,\infty)$ and let $Z\sim\operatorname{Poisson}(\gamma^2c_{\varphi}a^2/2)$. Then,
$$
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{Wass}}(E_s,Z) \leq 4\gamma^3c_{\varphi}^2 a^2 \mathbb{E}[W^2 \mathbf{1}\{W\geq t_s\}]
$$
for $s\geq 1$.
\item [b)] Assume that $\mathbb{E}[W^4]<\infty$ and let $N$ be a standard normal random variable. Then, there exist constants $C_{\mathrm{Wass}}',C_{\mathrm{K}}'\in(0,\infty)$ only depending on $\gamma$ and $\varphi$ such that, for all $s\geq 1$,
$$
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{Wass}}\bigg( \frac{E_s-\mathbb{E}[E_s]}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}[E_s]}}, N \bigg) \leq \frac{C_{\mathrm{Wass}}  R_s'}{\sqrt{s}}
$$
and
$$
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{K}}\bigg( \frac{E_s-\mathbb{E}[E_s]}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}[E_s]}}, N \bigg) \leq \frac{C_{\mathrm{K}}  R_s'}{\sqrt{s}}
$$
with 
\begin{align*}
R_s':= & \frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[W^3 \mathbf{1}\{W\geq t_s\}]}}{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[W \mathbf{1}\{W\geq t_s\}]}} + \sqrt{\mathbb{E}[W^4 \mathbf{1}\{W\geq t_s\}]} + \frac{2+2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[W^2 \mathbf{1}\{W\geq t_s\}]}^3}{\mathbb{E}[W \mathbf{1}\{W\geq t_s\}]}.
\end{align*}
\end{itemize}
\end{corollary}


Finally, we derive a compound Poisson approximation result for the total edge length of the hub-to-hub edges with midpoint in our observation window. Apart from the product kernel we additionally assume that the weights are Pareto distributed.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:hub-to-hub-edges2}
Assume that $\kappa(x_1,x_2)=x_1x_2$ and that $W\sim\operatorname{Pareto}(\beta)$ with $\beta>2$. Let $(t_s)_{s\geq 1}$ be such that $\sqrt{s}\mathbb{E}[W\mathbf{1}\{W\geq t_s\}]=a$ for some $a\in(0,\infty)$ and let
$$
F_s= \frac{t_s^{-\frac{2}{d}}}{2} \sum_{(x_1,x_2)\in\eta_{\neq}^2} \mathbf{1}\{x_1\leftrightarrow x_2\} \mathbf{1}\bigg\{ \frac{x_1+x_2}{2}\in \Gamma_s, W_{x_1},W_{x_2}\geq t_s\bigg\} \|x_1-x_2\|.
$$
Denote by $\zeta$ a Poisson process on $\mathbb{R}$ with the intensity measure
$$
\mathbf{L}(A) := \frac{\gamma^2(\beta-1)^2a^2}{2\beta^2} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}\{(W_1W_2)^{1/d}\|v\|\in A\} W_1W_2] \varphi(1/\|v\|^d) \, \mathrm{d}v
$$
for $A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})$ and let $Z:=\sum_{z\in\zeta} z$. Then,
$$
\mathrm{d_{TV}}(F_s,Z) \leq \frac{4\gamma^3 c_\varphi^2 a^2 \beta}{\beta-2}t_s^{2-\beta}
$$
for $s\geq 1$.
\end{theorem}



\section{Results for $U$-statistics of edge-marked Poisson processes}



\subsection{Edge-marked Poisson processes}\label{subsec:edge-marked_PP}

Although we are mostly interested in the random connection model, we derive general results for certain functionals and point processes constructed from so-called edge-marked Poisson processes. The idea of an edge-marked Poisson process is to take a Poisson process and to assign i.i.d.\ marks to all pairs of points. The random connection model can be derived from such an edge-marked Poisson process in the following way. We take the Poisson process of the vertices and equip each pair of vertices $x$ and $y$ with an independent random variable $U_{x,y}$ that is uniformly distributed on $[0,1]$. Then we connect $x$ and $y$ by an edge if and only if $U_{x,y}\leq \varphi(x,y)$, where $\varphi$ is the connection function.

In the following, we introduce edge-marked Poisson processes on a general measurable space. To this end let $(\mathbb{X},\mathcal{X})$ be a measurable space and let $\lambda$ be a $\sigma$-finite non-atomic measure on $\mathbb{X}$. For $\mathbb{X}$ and $\lambda$ we always assume that for each $n\in\mathbb{N}$ there exists a partition $(A_\ell^{(n)})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}$ of $\mathbb{X}$, i.e., $A^{(n)}_{\ell_1}\cap A^{(n)}_{\ell_2}=\emptyset$ for distinct $\ell_1,\ell_2\in\mathbb{N}$ and $\bigcup_{\ell=1}^\infty A^{(n)}_\ell=\mathbb{X}$, such that the following conditions are satisfied:
\begin{itemize}
\item For $x\in\mathbb{X}$ and $n\in\mathbb{N}$ let $A^{(n)}(x)$ be the unique $A^{(n)}_\ell$ such that $x\in A^{(n)}_\ell$. Then,
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:monotonicity_partition}
A^{(n)}(x)\subseteq A^{(m)}(x)
\end{equation}
for all $x\in\mathbb{X}$ and $m,n\in\mathbb{N}$ with $m\leq n$.
\item There exists a sequence $(\varepsilon_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ such that $\lim_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon_n=0$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:size_partition}
\lambda(A_\ell^{(n)}) \leq \frac{\varepsilon_n}{\ell}
\end{equation}
for all $\ell,n\in\mathbb{N}$.
\item There exists a measurable function $\operatorname{dist}:\mathbb{X}\times \mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)$ such that 
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:uniqueness_distances}
\lambda^3\left(\left\{(x,y,z)\in \mathbb{X}\times A^{(n)}_\ell\times A_\ell^{(n)}: \operatorname{dist}(x,y)=\operatorname{dist}(x,z)\right\}\right)=0
\end{equation}
for all $\ell,n\in\mathbb{N}$.
\end{itemize}

Let us briefly discuss choices of $\mathbb{X}$ and $\lambda$ that are relevant for studying the random connection model. For the stationary random connection model without weights one chooses $\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^d$ and $\lambda=\gamma\lambda_d$, where $\lambda_d$ is the $d$-dimensional Lebesgue measure and $\gamma>0$ is an intensity parameter. For the case of stationary weighted random connection models we let $\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^d\times [0,\infty)$ and $\lambda=\gamma\lambda_d\otimes \mathbb{W}$ with $\gamma$ and $\lambda_d$ as before and the weight distribution $\mathbb{W}$. In case one is only interested in the random connection model in some observation window $K$, one replaces $\mathbb{R}^d$ by $K$ and the Lebesgue measure by the restriction of the Lebesgue measure to $K$.  

Let $\eta$ be a Poisson process on $\mathbb{X}$ with the $\sigma$-finite non-atomic intensity measure $\lambda$. We assume that $\eta$ can be almost surely written as 
$$
\eta=\sum_{i\in I} \delta_{X_i}
$$
with random elements $(X_i)_{i\in I}$ on $\mathbb{X}$ and a random index set $I\subseteq\mathbb{N}$, where $\delta_x$ denotes the Dirac measure concentrated on $x$. 

Moreover, let $(\mathbb{M},\mathcal{M})$ be a measurable space with a probability measure $\mathbb{Q}$. Let $(\mathcal{M}_{i,j})_{i,j\in\mathbb{N},i\neq j}$ be a family of independent random elements on $\mathbb{M}$ distributed according to $\mathbb{Q}$. Now we assign the mark $\mathcal{M}_{i,j}$ to the pair $(X_i,X_j)$ for $i,j\in I$ with $i\neq j$. For $y,z\in\eta$ with $y\neq z$ we denote by $\mathcal{M}_{y,z}$ the mark of the pair $(y,z)$. Note that the pairs $(y,z)$ and $(z,y)$ have different marks.



\subsection{$U$-statistics and moment formulae}
For $k\in\mathbb{N}$ we use the short hand notation $[k]:=\{1,\hdots,k\}$ and define
$$
\mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}:=\{(u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j}: u_{i,j}\in \mathbb{M} \text{ for } i,j\in[k],i\neq j\}.
$$
In the following, we often denote the entries of $\mathbf{u}\in \mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}$ by $(u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j}$. Moreover let $\eta^k_{\neq}$ be the set of all $k$-tuples of distinct points of $\eta$.



For a measurable function $f: \mathbb{X}^k\times \mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}\to\mathbb{R}$ with $k\in\mathbb{N}$ we are interested in random variables of the following form
\begin{equation}\label{def:ustat}
	S=\sum_{(x_1,\hdots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\neq}} f(x_1,\hdots,x_k, (\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j\in[k], i\neq j}),
\end{equation}
which we call $U$-statistics of the edge-marked Poisson process. Since we sum also over all permutations of a fixed $k$-tuple of points, we can assume without loss of generality that $f$ is symmetric, i.e., 
$$
f(x_1,\hdots,x_k, (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j})=f(x_{\pi(1)},\hdots,x_{\pi(k)}, (u_{\pi(i),\pi(j)})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j})
$$
for all $x_1,\hdots,x_k\in\mathbb{X}^k$, $\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}$ and all permutations $\pi:[k]\to[k]$. Indeed, the value of $S$ does not change if we replace $f$ by its symmetrisation
$$
\widetilde{f}(x_1,\hdots,x_k, (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j}):=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\pi\in \mathrm{P}([k])}f(x_{\pi(1)},\hdots,x_{\pi(k)}, (u_{\pi(i),\pi(j)})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j})
$$
for $x_1,\hdots,x_k\in\mathbb{X}^k$ and $\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}$, where $\mathrm{P}([k])$ is the set of all permutations of $[k]$.
We often formulate assumptions on terms of the $U$-statistic
\begin{equation}\label{def-integ1}
	\overline{S}:=\sum_{(x_1,\hdots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\neq}} \left|f(x_1,\hdots,x_k, (\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j\in[k], i\neq j})\right|.
\end{equation}
Note that $S=\overline{S}$ if $f$ is non-negative.

We start with formulae for expectations and variances of $U$-statistics of edge-marked Poisson processes. From now on we always assume that the function $f$ is symmetric as discussed above.

\begin{prop}\label{prop:expectation_variance}
    % Let $f: \mathbb{X}^k\times \mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}\to\mathbb{R}$ with $k\in\mathbb{N}$ be a measurable function. 
    \begin{enumerate}
    \item[]
    \item[a)]
    Suppose $\E(\overline{S})<\infty$, then we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:generalised_Mecke_U-statistics}
\E S = \int_{\mathbb{X}^k}\int_{\mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}} f(x_1,\hdots,x_k,\mathbf{u}) \, \mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{k\times k}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^k(\mathrm{d}(x_1,\hdots,x_k)).
\end{equation}
\item[b)] Suppose $\E\left(\overline{S}^2\right)<\infty$, then we have 
\begin{align}\label{eqn:variance}
\Var S =\sum_{\ell=1}^k{k\choose \ell}^2\ell! &\int_{\mathbb{X}^{2k-\ell}}\int_{\mathbb{M}^{(2k-\ell)\times (2k-\ell)}_{\neq}} f(x_1,\hdots,x_k,(u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\ne j}) \nonumber\\
&f(x_1,\dots,x_{\ell},x_{k+1},\dots,x_{2k-\ell},(u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[\ell]\cup\{k+1,\dots,2k-\ell\},i\ne j}) )\\
&\mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{(2k-\ell)\times (2k-\ell)}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^{2k-\ell}(\mathrm{d}(x_1,\hdots,x_{2k-\ell})).\nonumber
\end{align}
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}
For higher moments, we need a few more notation about partitions. 

Let $A$ be a non-empty set. A partition $\sigma$ of $A$ is a collection $\{B_1,\dots,B_s\}$ of pairwise disjoint non-empty subsets $B_i\subseteq A$, called blocks, such that $B_1\cup\cdots\cup B_s=A$. The number $s$ of blocks in $\sigma$ is denoted by $|\sigma|$.
% For any $n\in\N$, we denote $[n]:=\{1,\dots,n\}$.
Let $t\in\N$ and $q\in\N$. Set $J_\ell:=\{(\ell,1),(\ell,2),\dots,(\ell,q)\}$, for $\ell=1,\dots,t.$ By $\Pi^t(q)$ we denote the set of all partitions $\sigma$ of $[t]\times[q]$ such that $|B\cap J_\ell|\le1$ for all $\ell\in[t]$ and $B\in\sigma$.

Every partition $\sigma\in\Pi^t(q)$ induces a partition $\sigma^*$ of $[t]$ in the following way: $i,j\in[t]$ are in the same block of $\sigma^*$ whenever there exists a block $B\in\sigma$ such that $|B\cap J_i|=|B\cap J_j|=1$. Let $\widetilde{\Pi}^t(q)$ be the collection of all partitions $\sigma\in\Pi^t(q)$ such that $|\sigma^*|=1$. Note that partitions in $\Pi^t(q)$ and $\widetilde{\Pi}^t(q)$ are known as non-flat and connected non-flat, respectively, see \cite[Chapter~4]{peccati2011}. 
We denote by $\overline{\Pi}^t(q)$ the collection of all partitions $\sigma\in\Pi^t(q)$ such that for each $\ell\in[t]$ there exists a block $B\in\sigma$ with $|B|\ge2$ and $B\cap J_\ell\ne\emptyset$.
%By $\Pi_{\ge2}(q_1,\dots,q_t)$ and $\widetilde{\Pi}_{\ge2}(q_1,\dots,q_t)$, we denote the sets of all $\sigma\in\Pi(q_1,\dots,q_t)$ and $\sigma\in\widetilde{\Pi}(q_1,\dots,q_t)$ such that $|B|\ge2$ for every $B\in\sigma$. In the case $q_1=\dots=q_t=q$, we write $\Pi^t(q)$, $\widetilde{\Pi}^t(q)$, $\Pi^t_{\ge2}(q)$, $\widetilde{\Pi}^t_{\ge2}(q)$ and $\overline{\Pi}^t(q)$ instead of $\Pi(q_1,\dots,q_t)$, $\widetilde{\Pi}(q_1,\dots,q_t)$, $\Pi_{\ge2}(q_1,\dots,q_t)$, $\widetilde{\Pi}_{\ge2}(q_1,\dots,q_t)$ and $\overline{\Pi}(q_1,\dots,q_t)$ respectively.

%\textcolor{blue}{Let $(S,\mathcal{S})$ be an arbitrary measurable space. For functions $f^{(\ell)}:S^{q}\to\R$, $\ell\in[t]$, we define their tensor product $\otimes_{\ell=1}^tf^{(\ell)}:S^{tq}\to\R$ by 
%$$\otimes_{\ell=1}^tf^{(\ell)}(x_1,\dots,x_{tq}):=\prod_{\ell=1}^tf^{(\ell)}(x_{(\ell-1)q+1},\dots,x_{\ell q}).$$
%For each $\sigma\in\Pi^t(q)$, the function $(\otimes_{\ell=1}^tf^{(\ell)})_\sigma:S^{|\sigma|}\to\mathbb{R}$ is obtained by replacing all variables that belong to the same block in $\sigma$ by a new common variable.}
%not needed anymore.
For any $f: \mathbb{X}^k\times \mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}\to\mathbb{R}$ with $k\in\mathbb{N}$, and for each $\sigma=\{b_1,\dots,b_{|\sigma|}\}\in\Pi^t(k)$, we denote $(f^{\otimes t})_{\sigma}:\mathbb{X}^{|\sigma|}\times \mathbb{M}^{|\sigma|\times |\sigma|}_{\neq}\to\mathbb{R}$ as 
\begin{align}
  (f^{\otimes t})_{\sigma}(x_1,\dots,x_{|\sigma|},(u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[|\sigma|],i\ne j}):=\prod_{\ell=1}^t f(x^{(\ell)}_1,\dots,x^{(\ell)}_k,(u^{(\ell)}_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\ne j}),
\end{align}
with $x^{(\ell)}_{t}:=x_i$ if $(\ell,t)\in b_i$, and $u^{(\ell)}_{t,s}:=u_{i,j}$ if $(\ell,t)\in b_i$ and $(\ell,s)\in b_j$.

\begin{prop}\label{prop:moments}
    Suppose that $\E\left(\overline{S}^{\ell}\right)<\infty$ for some $\ell\in\N$, then 
    \begin{eqnarray}
\E[S^{\ell}]&=&\sum_{\pi\in\Pi^\ell(k)}\int_{\mathbb{X}^{|\pi|}}\int_{\mathbb{M}^{|\pi|\times|\pi|}_{\ne}}(f^{\otimes\ell})_{\pi}(\bm{x},\bm{u})\mathbb{Q}^{|\pi|\times|\pi|}_{\ne}(\mathrm{d}\bm{u})\lambda^{|\pi|}(\mathrm{d}\bm{x}),\label{momeq-1}\\
\E[(S-\E S)^{\ell}]&=&\sum_{\pi\in\overline{\Pi}^\ell(k)}\int_{\mathbb{X}^{|\pi|}}\int_{\mathbb{M}^{|\pi|\times|\pi|}_{\ne}}(f^{\otimes\ell})_{\pi}(\bm{x},\bm{u})\mathbb{Q}^{|\pi|\times|\pi|}_{\ne}(\mathrm{d}\bm{u})\lambda^{|\pi|}(\mathrm{d}\bm{x}),\label{momeq-2}\\
\cum_\ell(S)&=&\sum_{\pi\in\widetilde{\Pi}^\ell(k)}\int_{\mathbb{X}^{|\pi|}}\int_{\mathbb{M}^{|\pi|\times|\pi|}_{\ne}}(f^{\otimes\ell})_{\pi}(\bm{x},\bm{u})\mathbb{Q}^{|\pi|\times|\pi|}_{\ne}(\mathrm{d}\bm{u})\lambda^{|\pi|}(\mathrm{d}\bm{x}),\label{momeq-3}
\end{eqnarray}
where $\cum_\ell(S)$ stands for the $\ell$-th cumulant of $S$.
\end{prop}
% For a function $f:\mathbb{X}^k\times\mathbb{M}_{\neq}^{k\times k}\to\mathbb{R}$ with $k\in\mathbb{N}$ we have
% \begin{equation}\label{eqn:generalised_Mecke_U-statistics}
% \E\sum_{(x_1,\hdots,x_k)\in\eta_{\neq}^k} f(x_1,\hdots,x_k,(M_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j}) = \int_{\mathbb{X}^k}\int_{\mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}} f(x_1,\hdots,x_k,\mathbf{u}) \, \mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{k\times k}(d\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^k(d(x_1,\hdots,x_k))
% \end{equation}
% if the integral on the right-hand side is absolutely integrable.

\subsection{Normal approximation}
% For $\pi\in\Pi^\ell(k)$, we define 
\begin{theorem}\label{normalapp1}
Let $f: \mathbb{X}^k\times \mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}\to\mathbb{R}$ be a measurable function 
% satisfying $\supp(f)\subset B\times\mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}$ with some bounded set $B\in \mathcal{X}^k$
and 
$$S:=\sum_{(x_1,\hdots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\neq}} f(x_1,\hdots,x_k, (\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j\in[k], i\neq j}).$$ 
Suppose that $\E[\overline{S}^2]<\infty$ with $\overline{S}$ given in \eqref{def-integ1}. Let $N$ denote a standard normal random variable.
Then, we have
\begin{eqnarray}
	\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{Wass}}\left(\frac{S-\E S}{\sqrt{\Var S}},N\right)&\le&2k^{7/2}\sum_{1\le i\le j\le k}\frac{\sqrt{M_{ij}(f)}}{\Var S},\label{norbound-1}\\
	\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{K}}\left(\frac{S-\E S}{\sqrt{\Var S}},N\right)&\le&19k^5\sum_{i,j=1}^k\frac{\sqrt{M_{ij}(f)}}{\Var S},\label{norbound-2}
\end{eqnarray}
where 
% \begin{eqnarray*}
%     M_{ij}(f)&:=&\sum_{\pi\in\widetilde{\Pi}_{\ge2}(i,i,j,j)}\int_{\mathbb{X}^{|\pi|}} \int_{\mathbb{M}^{|\pi|\times |\pi|}_{\neq}} \left(\overline{f}_i\otimes\overline{f}_i\otimes\overline{f}_j\otimes\overline{f}_j\right)_{\pi}(\bm{x},\bm{u}) \, \mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{|\pi|\times |\pi|}(\mathrm{d}\bm{u}) \, \lambda^{|\pi|}(\mathrm{d}\bm{x}),
% \end{eqnarray*}
\begin{eqnarray*}
    M_{ij}(f)&:=&\sum_{\substack{\pi\in\widetilde{\Pi}^4(k)\\s_1(\pi)=s_2(\pi)=k-i\\s_3(\pi)=s_4(\pi)=k-j}}\int_{\mathbb{X}^{|\pi|}} \int_{\mathbb{M}^{|\pi|\times |\pi|}_{\neq}} \left(|f|^{\otimes 4}\right)_{\pi}(\bm{x},\bm{u}) \, \mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{|\pi|\times |\pi|}(\mathrm{d}\bm{u}) \, \lambda^{|\pi|}(\mathrm{d}\bm{x})
\end{eqnarray*}
for $i,j\in[k]$ with %$\bm{x}=(x_1,\hdots,x_{|\pi|})$, $\bm{u}=(u_{\imath,\jmath})_{1\le\imath\ne\jmath\le|\pi|}$, and 
$$s_u(\pi):=|\{B\in\pi:B\subseteq J_u\}|$$
for $u\in[4]$ and $\pi\in\Pi^4(k)$.
\end{theorem}

Next we consider the special case of $U$-statistics of order two. We use the notation $\mathbf{u}_{\{\ell_1,\ell_2\}}:=(u_{i,j})_{i,j\in\{\ell_1,\ell_2\}, i\neq j}$ for $\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{M}^{m\times m}_{\neq}$ with $m\geq 2$ and $\ell_1,\ell_2\in\{1.\hdots,m\}$ with $\ell_1\neq \ell_2$.

\begin{corollary}\label{normalapp1k2}
Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem \ref{normalapp1} are satisfied for $k=2$. Then,
\begin{align*}
	\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{Wass}}\left(\frac{S-\E S}{\sqrt{\Var S}},N\right) & \le \frac{32 \Big(\sqrt{\widetilde{M}_1(f)} + (1+\sqrt{3}) \sqrt{\widetilde{M}_2(f)} + \sqrt{\widetilde{M}_3(f)}\Big)}{\Var S},\\
	\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{K}}\left(\frac{S-\E S}{\sqrt{\Var S}},N\right) & \le \frac{608 \Big(\sqrt{\widetilde{M}_1(f)} + (2+\sqrt{3}) \sqrt{\widetilde{M}_2(f)} + \sqrt{\widetilde{M}_3(f)}\Big)}{\Var S}
\end{align*}
with
\begin{align*}
\widetilde{M}_{1}(f) & := 16\int_{\mathbb{X}^5} \int_{\mathbb{M}_{\neq}^{5\times 5}} \prod_{i=2}^5 |f(x_1,x_i,\mathbf{u}_{\{1,i\}})| \, \mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{5\times 5}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^5(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}), \allowdisplaybreaks \\
\widetilde{M}_{2}(f) & := 16\int_{\mathbb{X}^4} \int_{\mathbb{M}_{\neq}^{4\times 4}} |f(x_1,x_2,\mathbf{u}_{\{1,2\}}) f(x_1,x_3,\mathbf{u}_{\{1,3\}}) f(x_1,x_4,\mathbf{u}_{\{1,4\}})^2| \, \mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{4\times 4}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^4(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}) \\
& \quad +16\int_{\mathbb{X}^4} \int_{\mathbb{M}_{\neq}^{4\times 4}} |f(x_1,x_2,\mathbf{u}_{\{1,2\}}) f(x_3,x_4,\mathbf{u}_{\{3,4\}}) f(x_1,x_3,\mathbf{u}_{\{1,3\}})^2| \, \mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{4\times 4}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^4(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}) \\
& \quad +32\int_{\mathbb{X}^5} \int_{\mathbb{M}_{\neq}^{5\times 5}} |f(x_1,x_2,\mathbf{u}_{\{1,2\}}) f(x_3,x_4,\mathbf{u}_{\{3,4\}}) f(x_1,x_5,\mathbf{u}_{\{1,5\}})  f(x_3,x_5,\mathbf{u}_{\{3,5\}})| \\
& \hspace{3cm} \mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{5\times 5}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^5(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}), \allowdisplaybreaks\\
\widetilde{M}_{3}(f) & := 144 \int_{\mathbb{X}^3} \int_{\mathbb{M}_{\neq}^{3\times 3}} f(x_1,x_2,\mathbf{u}_{\{1,2\}})^2 f(x_1,x_3,\mathbf{u}_{\{2,3\}})^2 \, \mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{3\times 3}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^3(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}) \\
& \quad + 8 \int_{\mathbb{X}^2} \int_{\mathbb{M}_{\neq}^{2\times 2}} f(x_1,x_2,\mathbf{u}_{\{1,2\}})^4 \, \mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{2\times 2}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^2(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}).
\end{align*}
\end{corollary}
\subsection{Poisson process approximation}

For $k\in\mathbb{N}$ with $k\geq2$ and a locally compact second countable Hausdorff space $\mathbb{Y}$ with Borel $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{Y}$ we consider a measurable symmetric function $g: \operatorname{dom} g\to\mathbb{Y}$ such that $\operatorname{dom} g\subseteq \mathbb{X}^k\times \mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}$ is also measurable. Moreover, we define
$$
{\operatorname{dom}'} g := \left\{( x_1, \ldots, x_k)\in\mathbb{X}^k : \text{ there exists a } \mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq} \text{ such that } \left( x_1, \ldots, x_k, \mathbf{u} \right) \in  \operatorname{dom} g\right\}.
$$
In the following, we consider the point process
\begin{equation}\label{definition_xi}
	\xi:= \frac{1}{k!}\sum_{(x_1, \ldots, x_k) \in \eta^k_{\neq}\cap  {\operatorname{dom}'} g}  \delta_{g \left( x_1, \ldots, x_k, (\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j\in[k], i \neq j} \right)}.
\end{equation}
The intensity measure $\mathbf{L}$ of $\xi$ is given by
$$
\mathbf{L}(A):= \mathbb{E} \xi(A) =\frac{1}{k!} \int_{\operatorname{dom} g} \mathbf{1}\{ g(x_1,\hdots,x_k,\mathbf{u})\in A \} \, \lambda^k\otimes \mathbb{Q}_{\neq}^{k\times k}(\mathrm{d} (x_1,\hdots,x_k,\mathbf{u})) 
$$
for $A\in\mathcal{Y}$.

\begin{theorem} \label{Poisson_process_approximation}
Let $\xi$ be the point process given in \eqref{definition_xi} and assume that $\E \xi(\mathbb{Y})^2<\infty$. Let $\zeta$ be a Poisson process with finite intensity measure $\mathbf{M}$. Then,
    \begin{align*}
        \mathrm{d}_{\rm KR}({\xi}, \zeta) & \leq \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{M}) + 2 \big( \E \xi(\mathbb{Y})^2 - (\E \xi(\mathbb{Y}))^2 - \E \xi(\mathbb{Y}) \big) \\
        & \leq \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{M}) + \frac{2^{k+1}}{k!} \, r(\operatorname{dom} g),
    \end{align*}
where
\begin{align*}
  &  r(\operatorname{dom} g) := \max_{1 \leq \ell \leq k-1} 
\int_{\mathbb{X}^{2k-\ell}} \int_{\mathbb{M}^{(2k-\ell)\times (2k-\ell)}_{\neq}}  
\mathbf{1}\big\{ ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k , (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j} ) \in \operatorname{dom} g  \big\}  \\
&  \hspace{3cm} \times \mathbf{1}\big\{ ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell, {x}_{k+1}, \ldots, {x}_{2k-\ell}, (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in [\ell]\cup\{k+1,\hdots,2k-\ell\}, i\neq j} ) \in \operatorname{dom} g \big\} \\
& \hspace{3cm} \times  \mathbb{Q}^{(2k-\ell)\times(2k-\ell)}_{\neq} (\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^{2k-\ell}(\mathrm{d}(x_1,\hdots,x_{2k-\ell})).
\end{align*}  
\end{theorem}

Next we provide some corollaries of Theorem \ref{Poisson_process_approximation}. For $g: \operatorname{dom} g \to\mathbb{Y}$ as above and $B\in\mathcal{Y}$ we define the $U$-statistic
$$
T:=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{(x_1,\hdots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\neq}\cap \operatorname{dom}'g} \mathbf{1}\{g(x_1,\hdots,x_k,(\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j})\in B\}.
$$

\begin{cor}\label{cor:Poisson_approximation}
Assume that $\mathbb{E}[T^2]<\infty$ and let $Z$ be a Poisson distributed random variable with parameter $a\in[0,\infty)$. Then,
\begin{align*}
	\mathrm{d}_{\rm Wass}(T,Z) & \leq |\mathbb{E}[T]-a| + 2 ( \mathbb{E}[T^2]- \mathbb{E}[T] -(\mathbb{E}[T])^2 ) \\
& \leq |\mathbb{E}[T]-a| + \frac{2^{k+1}}{k!} r(B)
\end{align*}
with
\begin{align*}
  &  r(B) := \max_{1 \leq \ell \leq k-1} 
\int_{\mathbb{X}^{2k-\ell}} \int_{\mathbb{M}^{(2k-\ell)\times (2k-\ell)}_{\neq}}  
\mathbf{1}\big\{ g({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k , (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j} ) \in B \big\}  \\
&  \hspace{3cm} \times \mathbf{1}\big\{ g({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell, {x}_{k+1}, \ldots, {x}_{2k-\ell}, (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in [\ell]\cup\{k+1,\hdots,2k-\ell\}, i\neq j} ) \in B \big\} \\
& \hspace{3cm} \times  \mathbb{Q}^{(2k-\ell)\times(2k-\ell)}_{\neq} (\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^{2k-\ell}(\mathrm{d}(x_1,\hdots,x_{2k-\ell})).
\end{align*}  
\end{cor}


Let $g: \mathbb{X}^k\times\mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}\to\mathbb{R}$ be a measurable symmetric function. Define the $U$-statistic
\begin{equation}\label{definition_U}
	U := \frac{1}{k!}\sum_{(x_1,\hdots,x_k)\in\eta^k_{\neq}} g(x_1,\hdots,x_k,(\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j}). 
\end{equation}
Moreover, let
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{L}(A) := \frac{1}{k!} \, \mathbb{E} \sum_{(x_1, \ldots, x_k) \in \eta_{\neq}^k} 
\mathbf{1}\left\{ g(x_1, \ldots, x_k, (\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j\in[k], i \neq j}) \in A \setminus \{0\} \right\}
\end{align*}
for $A \in \mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})$, where $\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})$ is the Borel $\sigma$-algebra on $\mathbb{R}$. We get the following result.

\begin{cor}\label{compound}
For a Poisson process  \( \zeta \) on \( \mathbb{R} \) with a finite intensity measure \( \mathbf{M} \) let
\[
Y := \sum_{x \in \zeta} x.
\]
Then,
\begin{align*}
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(U, Y) \le \mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathbf{L}, \mathbf{M}) + \frac{2^{k+1}}{k!} \, r_0
\end{align*}
with
\begin{align*}
  &  r_0 := \max_{1 \leq \ell \leq k-1} 
\int_{\mathbb{X}^{2k-\ell}} \int_{\mathbb{M}^{(2k-\ell)\times (2k-\ell)}_{\neq}}  
\mathbf{1}\big\{ g({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k , (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j} )\neq 0 \big\}  \\
&  \hspace{3cm} \times \mathbf{1}\big\{ g({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell, {x}_{k+1}, \ldots, {x}_{2k-\ell}, (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in [\ell]\cup\{k+1,\hdots,2k-\ell\}, i\neq j} ) \neq 0 \big\} \\
& \hspace{3cm} \times  \mathbb{Q}^{(2k-\ell)\times(2k-\ell)}_{\neq} (\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^{2k-\ell}(\mathrm{d}(x_1,\hdots,x_{2k-\ell})).
\end{align*}
\end{cor}


\iffalse
Next we compare the distribution of the random variable $U$ introduced in \eqref{definition_U} with an $\alpha$-stable distribution. To this end we fix $\alpha\in(0,1)$ and let
$$
g_\alpha (\mathbf{x}_m) :=  \operatorname{sign}(g(\mathbf{x}_m)) g(\mathbf{x}_m)^{-\alpha}
$$
for $\mathbf{x}_{\textcolor{cyan}{m}}\in \mathbb{X}^k\times\mathbb{M}^{k\times k}_{\neq}$, where $\operatorname{sign}(u) = \mathbf{1}\{u \ge 0\} - \mathbf{1}\{u < 0\}$ for $u\in\mathbb{R}$.
We define
\begin{align*}
	\mathbf{L}^{(\alpha)}(A) & := \frac{1}{k!} \, \mathbb{E} \sum_{(x_1, \ldots, x_k) \in \eta_{\neq}^k} \mathbf{1}\{ g(\mathbf{x}_m)\neq 0 \}  \mathbf{1}\{ g_\alpha(x_1, \ldots, x_k, (\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j\in[k], i \neq j})\in A \}
\end{align*}
for $A \in \mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})$.

\begin{cor}\label{approximation_alpha-stable}
Let \( \alpha \in (0, 1) \), and let \( \mathbf{M} \) be either the Lebesgue measure on \( \mathbb{R} \) or its restriction to \( (0,\infty) \). Define
\[
Y := \sum_{x \in \zeta} \operatorname{sign}(x) |x|^{-1/\alpha},
\]
where \( \zeta \) is a Poisson process with intensity measure \( \mathbf{M}\). Assume that there exist functions \( g_1, g_2, g_3 \colon (0,\infty) \to [0,\infty) \) such that, for every \( a > 0 \),
\begin{align*}
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathbf{L}^{(\alpha)}|_{[-a,a]}, \mathbf{M}|_{[-a,a]}) &\le g_1(a),
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
&\max_{1 \leq \ell \leq k-1} 
\int_{\mathbb{X}^{2k-\ell}} \int_{\mathbb{M}^{(2k-\ell)\times (2k-\ell)}_{\neq}}  
\mathbf{1}\big\{ g({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k , (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j} )\neq 0 \big\}\mathbf{1}\big\{ g_\alpha({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k , (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j} )\in [-a,a] \big\}  \\
&  \hspace{3cm} \times  \mathbf{1}\big\{ g({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell, {x}_{k+1}, \ldots, {x}_{2k-\ell}, (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in [\ell]\cup\{k+1,\hdots,2k-\ell\}, i\neq j} ) \neq 0 \big\}\\
& \hspace{3cm} \times \mathbf{1}\big\{ g_\alpha({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell, {x}_{k+1}, \ldots, {x}_{2k-\ell}, (u_{i,j})_{i,j\in [\ell]\cup\{k+1,\hdots,2k-\ell\}, i\neq j} ) \in [-a,a] \big\}\\
& \hspace{3cm} \times  \mathbb{Q}^{(2k-\ell)\times(2k-\ell)}_{\neq} (\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}) \, \lambda^{2k-\ell}(\mathrm{d}(x_1,\hdots,x_{2k-\ell}))\\
&\le g_2(a),
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
& \frac{1}{k!} \, \mathbb{E} \sum_{(x_1, \ldots, x_k) \in \eta^k_{\neq}} \mathbf{1}\{|g(x_1, \ldots, x_k,(\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j\in[k], i \neq j})| < a^{-1/\alpha}\} |g(x_1, \ldots, x_k,(\mathcal{M}_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j \in[k], i \neq j})| \\
&\le g_3(a).
\end{align*}
Then there exists a constant \( C > 0 \), depending only on \( \alpha \) and \( k \), such that
\[
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{K}}(U, Y) \le C b,
\]
where
\[
b := \inf_{a > 0} \max\left\{ a^{1/2 - 1/(2\alpha)},\; g_1(a),\; g_2(a),\; \sqrt{g_3(a)} \right\}.
\]
\end{cor}

\begin{proof}
The proof is analogously to the derivation of Theorem 7.8 from Theorem 3.1 in \cite{dst}. 
\end{proof}
\fi

\iffalse\subsection{Poisson process approximation (old)}
Now we consider the functional analogue of the previous section. Define for a symmetric and measurable function $f\colon \operatorname{dom} f \to Y$ ($Y$ is some general space) the process
\begin{align}\label{originalPproc}
     \xi:= \frac{1}{k!}\sum_{(x_1, \ldots, x_k) \in \eta^k_{\neq}\cap  {\operatorname{dom}'} f} \delta_{f \left( x_1, \ldots, x_k, (W_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j =1,\ldots, k, i \neq j} \right)} ,
 \end{align}
where we denote
$${\operatorname{dom}'} f := \left\{( x_1, \ldots, x_k) : \left( x_1, \ldots, x_k, (W_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j =1,\ldots, k, i \neq j} \right) \in  \operatorname{dom} f\right\} . $$
As before note that $\xi$ is not induced by a Poisson process. Therefore we define
$$ \hat{\xi}:= \frac{1}{k!}\sum_{(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k) \in \hat{\eta}^k_{\neq} \cap  \operatorname{dom} f} \delta_{f \left( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k, (\hat{W}_{\hat{x}_i,\hat{x}_j}) \right)} =:  \sum_{(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k) \in \hat{\eta}^k_{\neq}\cap  \operatorname{dom} f} \delta_{\hat{f} ( \hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k )},$$
which on the other hand is induced by a Poisson process. Note that the intensity measure of $\xi$ is given by
\begin{align*}
    L(A) & = \mathbb{E}[\xi(A)] = \frac{1}{k!} \mathbb{E}\left[ \sum_{(x_1, \ldots, x_k) \in \eta^k_{\neq}} \mathbf{1}_{\{ f(x_1, \ldots, x_k, (W_{x_i,x_j})_{i,j =1,\ldots, k, i \neq j}) \in A\}}\right] \\
    & = \frac{1}{k!}  \int_{\operatorname{dom} f}  \mathbf{1}_{\{ f(x_1, \ldots, x_k, w) \in A\}} K^k(d(x_1, \ldots, x_k)) d\mathbb{Q}^{\frac{k (k-1)}{2}} (dw),
\end{align*}
and the one of $\hat{\xi}$ is given by
\begin{align*}
    &\hat{L}(A)  = \mathbb{E}[\hat{\xi(A)}] = \frac{1}{k!} \mathbb{E}\left[ \sum_{(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k) \in \eta^k_{\neq}} \mathbf{1}_{\{ \hat{f}(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k) \in A\}}\right] \\
    & = \frac{1}{k!}  \int_{\operatorname{dom} f}  \mathbf{1}_{\{  \hat{f}(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k) \in A\}} \hat{K}^k(d(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k)) \\
    &  = \frac{1}{k!}  \int_{\operatorname{dom} f} \mathbf{1}_{\{  ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k ) \in \Delta_{k} \} }  \mathbf{1}_{\{  \hat{f}(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k) \in A\}} \hat{K}^k(d(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k)) \\
    & \quad +  \frac{1}{k!}  \int_{\operatorname{dom} f} \mathbf{1}_{\{  ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k ) \notin \Delta_{k} \} }  \mathbf{1}_{\{  \hat{f}(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k) \in A\}} \hat{K}^k(d(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k))
    =: \operatorname{I} +  \operatorname{II} .
\end{align*}

Then we have
$$ \operatorname{I}  \mathrel{\mathop{\to}\limits_{\varepsilon \downarrow 0}} 0
$$
and

\begin{align*}
     \operatorname{II}  & = \frac{1}{k!}  \int_{\operatorname{dom} f} \mathbf{1}_{\{  ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k ) \notin \Delta_{k} \} }  \mathbf{1}_{\{  {f}({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k, w) \in A\}} {K}^k(d({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k))d\mathbb{Q}^{\frac{k (k-1)}{2}} (dw) \\
     &  \mathrel{\mathop{\to}\limits_{\varepsilon \downarrow 0}}\frac{1}{k!}  \int_{\operatorname{dom} f}   \mathbf{1}_{\{  {f}({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k, w) \in A\}} {K}^k(d({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k))d\mathbb{Q}^{\frac{k (k-1)}{2}} (dw) ,
\end{align*}
both uniformly in $A$, if the intensity measure $K \otimes \mathbb{Q}$ is finite (we set $t=1$ above). Therefore, using \cite[Theorem 3.1]{dst} we obtain the following functional version of Theorem \ref{P1}.

\begin{theorem} \label{PPA}
    Let $\xi$ be the point process given in \eqref{originalPproc}. Let $\zeta$ be a Poisson process with finite intensity measure $M$. Assume either that
    \begin{itemize}
        \item[(i)] the intensity measure $K \otimes \mathbb{Q}$ is finite
    \end{itemize}
    or
    \begin{itemize}
        \item[(ii)] $\mathbb{P} (\exists r_0>0 \,: \, \hat{\eta}( \mathbb{R}^d \times \cdot) = \hat{\eta}( (B(0,r) \times \cdot) \, \forall r \geq r_0 ) = 1$.
    \end{itemize}
    Then it holds
    \begin{align*}
        d_{\rm KR}({\xi}, \zeta) \leq d_{TV}(L,M) + \frac{2^{k+1}}{k!} \, r(\operatorname{dom} f),
    \end{align*}
for
\begin{align*}
  &  r(\operatorname{dom} f) := \max_{1 \leq \ell \leq k-1} 
\int \int_{X^\ell}  
\int_{X^{k - \ell}}\int_{X^{k - \ell}} \mathbf{1}\big(  ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k , w ) \in \operatorname{dom} f  \big)  \\
&  \times \mathbf{1}\big( ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell, {x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, {x}'_{k}, w ) \in \operatorname{dom} f \big){K}^{k - \ell}(d({x}_{\ell+1}, \ldots, {x}_k)) \\
&  \times  {K}^{k - \ell}(d({x}'_{\ell+1}, \ldots, {x}'_k)) {K}^\ell(d({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell)) d\mathbb{Q}^{\frac{(2k - \ell) (2k -\ell-1)}{2}} (dw) .
\end{align*}
  
\end{theorem}







\subsubsection{Poisson approximation}

We can employ Theorem~\ref{Poisson_process_approximation} %\cite[Theorem 7.1]{dst} 
in order to bound
$$d_W (\hat{S}_t,Z) $$
for a Poisson distributed random variable $Z$ with mean $\lambda\in[0,\infty)$. By the latter theorem, we have

\[
d_W(\hat{S}, Z) \leq \left| \mathbb{E}[\hat{S}] - \lambda \right| + \frac{2^{k+1}}{k!} \, r_t(B)
\]
for
\begin{align*}
    r_t(B) := \max_{1 \leq \ell \leq k-1} 
\int_{\hat{X}^\ell} & \left( 
\int_{\hat{X}^{k - \ell}} 
\mathbf{1}\big( \hat{f}_t ( \hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k ) \in B \big) \, 
\hat{K}_t^{k - \ell}(d(x_{\ell+1}, \ldots, x_k)) 
\right)^2 \\
& \times \hat{K}_t^\ell(d(x_1, \ldots, x_\ell)),
\end{align*}
where $\hat{K}_t = t \hat{K}$ denotes the intensity measure of the Poisson point process $\hat{\eta}$ (and $\hat{K}$ is a $\sigma$-finite measure on the mark space). We write
\begin{align*}
   & \int_{\hat{X}^\ell}  \left( 
\int_{\hat{X}^{k - \ell}} 
\mathbf{1}\big( \hat{f}_t ( \hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k ) \in B \big) \, 
\hat{K}_t^{k - \ell}(d(\hat{x}_{\ell+1}, \ldots, \hat{x}_k)) 
\right)^2  \hat{K}_t^\ell(d(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_\ell)) \\
& = \int_{\hat{X}^\ell}  
\int_{\hat{X}^{k - \ell}}\int_{\hat{X}^{k - \ell}} 
\mathbf{1}\big( \hat{f}_t ( \hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k ) \in B \big) \,  \mathbf{1}\big( \hat{f}_t ( \hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_\ell, \hat{x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, \hat{x}'_{k} ) \in B \big) \\
& \qquad \times \hat{K}_t^{k - \ell}(d(\hat{x}_{\ell+1}, \ldots, \hat{x}_k)) \hat{K}_t^{k - \ell}(d(\hat{x}'_{\ell+1}, \ldots, \hat{x}'_k))\hat{K}_t^\ell(d(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_\ell))\\
& = \int_{\hat{X}^\ell}  
\int_{\hat{X}^{k - \ell}}\int_{\hat{X}^{k - \ell}} 
\mathbf{1}\big( \hat{f}_t ( \hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k ) \in B \big) \,  \mathbf{1}\big( \hat{f}_t ( \hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_\ell, \hat{x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, \hat{x}'_{k} ) \in B \big) \\
& \qquad \times  \mathbf{1}\big(  ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k, {x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, {x}'_{k} ) \in \Delta_{2k-\ell} \big) \\
& \qquad \times \hat{K}_t^{k - \ell}(d(\hat{x}_{\ell+1}, \ldots, \hat{x}_k)) \hat{K}_t^{k - \ell}(d(\hat{x}'_{\ell+1}, \ldots, \hat{x}'_k))\hat{K}_t^\ell(d(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_\ell)) \\
 & + \int_{X^\ell}  
\int_{\hat{X}^{k - \ell}}\int_{\hat{X}^{k - \ell}} 
\mathbf{1}\big( \hat{f}_t ( \hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k ) \in B \big) \,  \mathbf{1}\big( \hat{f} _t( \hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_\ell, \hat{x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, \hat{x}'_{k} ) \in B \big) \\
& \qquad \times  \mathbf{1}\big(  ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k, {x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, {x}'_{k} ) \notin \Delta_{2k-\ell} \big) \\
& \qquad \times \hat{K}_t^{k - \ell}(d(\hat{x}_{\ell+1}, \ldots, \hat{x}_k)) \hat{K}_t^{k - \ell}(d(\hat{x}'_{\ell+1}, \ldots, \hat{x}'_k))\hat{K}_t^\ell(d(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_\ell))
\end{align*}
with 
$$\Delta_{2k-\ell} := \left\{ (y_1, \ldots, y_{2k-\ell}) : \exists l, \,\, i,j \in \{1, \ldots, 2k-\ell \}, i \neq j : y_i,y_j \in A_l \right\} .$$
Next we write
\begin{align*}
  &  \int_{\hat{X}^\ell}  
\int_{\hat{X}^{k - \ell}}\int_{\hat{X}^{k - \ell}} 
\mathbf{1}\big( \hat{f}_t ( \hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_k ) \in B \big) \,  \mathbf{1}\big( \hat{f}_t ( \hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_\ell, \hat{x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, \hat{x}'_{k} ) \in B \big) \\
& \qquad \times  \mathbf{1}\big(  ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k, {x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, {x}'_{k} ) \notin \Delta_{2k-\ell} \big) \\
& \qquad \times \hat{K}_t^{k - \ell}(d(\hat{x}_{\ell+1}, \ldots, \hat{x}_k)) \hat{K}_t^{k - \ell}(d(\hat{x}'_{\ell+1}, \ldots, \hat{x}'_k))\hat{K}_t^\ell(d(\hat{x}_1, \ldots, \hat{x}_\ell)) \\
& = \mathbb{E} \Bigg[ \int_{X^\ell}  
\int_{X^{k - \ell}}\int_{X^{k - \ell}} 
\mathbf{1}\big(  ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k, {x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, {x}'_{k} ) \notin \Delta_{2k-\ell} \big) \,  \\
& \qquad \times \mathbf{1}\big( {f}_t ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k , W_{x_i,x_j} ) \in B \big)\mathbf{1}\big( {f}_t ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell, {x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, {x}'_{k}, \tilde{W}_{i,j} ) \in B \big) \\
& \qquad \times {K}_t^{k - \ell}(d({x}_{\ell+1}, \ldots, {x}_k)) {K}_t^{k - \ell}(d({x}'_{\ell+1}, \ldots, {x}'_k)) {K}_t^\ell(d({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell)) \Bigg] ,
\end{align*}
where now $K_t = tK$ denotes the intensity measure of $\eta$, and the random variables $\tilde{W}_{i,j}$ above are taken to be ${W}_{x_i,x_j}$ if $i,j \in [\ell]^2$ and $\overline{W}_{i,j}$ if $i,j \in [k]^2 \setminus [\ell]^2$ with $\overline{W}_{i,j}$ being an independent copy of the weight(s). Note that if $\mathbb{Q}$ denotes the distribution of the weights, we can write

\begin{align*}
   & \mathbb{E} \Bigg[ \int_{X^\ell}  
\int_{X^{k - \ell}}\int_{X^{k - \ell}} 
\mathbf{1}\big(  ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k, {x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, {x}'_{k} ) \notin \Delta_{2k-\ell} \big) \,  \\
& \qquad \times \mathbf{1}\big( {f}_t ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k , W_{x_i,x_j} ) \in B \big)\mathbf{1}\big( {f}_t ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell, {x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, {x}'_{k}, \tilde{W}_{i,j} ) \in B \big) \\
& \qquad \times {K}_t^{k - \ell}(d({x}_{\ell+1}, \ldots, {x}_k)) {K}_t^{k - \ell}(d({x}'_{\ell+1}, \ldots, {x}'_k)) {K}_t^\ell(d({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell)) \Bigg] \\
& = \int \int_{X^\ell}  
\int_{X^{k - \ell}}\int_{X^{k - \ell}} 
\mathbf{1}\big(  ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k, {x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, {x}'_{k} ) \notin \Delta_{2k-\ell} \big) \,  \\
&  \times \mathbf{1}\big( {f}_t ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_k , w ) \in B \big)\mathbf{1}\big( {f}_t ( {x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell, {x}'_{\ell+1} \ldots, {x}'_{k}, w ) \in B \big) \\
&  \times {K}_t^{k - \ell}(d({x}_{\ell+1}, \ldots, {x}_k)) {K}_t^{k - \ell}(d({x}'_{\ell+1}, \ldots, {x}'_k)) {K}_t^\ell(d({x}_1, \ldots, {x}_\ell)) d\mathbb{Q}^{\frac{(2k - \ell) (2k -\ell-1)}{2}} (dw).
\end{align*}

We obtain the following result regarding Poisson approximation for the original U-statistics.

\begin{theorem} \label{P1}
